That cannot be the objective of the European Union, it is not, and that is why we are responsible in this debate.

ParlaMint-AT_2008-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00063_d8e15058	Madam President. Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! The proposal today is to conclude a double taxation agreement with Turkey. The need arises from the revision of the existing agreement that important principles and individual points of the OECD pattern agreement will no longer be met and will result in disadvantages for Austria. Over a long period of time it has been negotiated with Turkey, which was particularly surprising, because Turkey is also an OECD member country and one could therefore assume that this is fulfilled from the outset. Nevertheless, it has finally succeeded in concluding this agreement and in creating a significant improvement for the investment climate, in particular for the Austrian investment construction companies, which are active in the engineering field in particular. The economic interests of Austria will again be able to be used optimally. I therefore believe that it is a useful thing to decide today on this double taxation agreement, which follows the OECD pattern agreement.

But I would like to address these last points in order to raise the question of a highly controversial project, namely the Ilisu dam. In the run-up to the state visit of Federal President Fischer, the Greens have again called for the Austro-Austrian Control Bank to withdraw from this project, since the low international standards imposed by the OeKB themselves are not met: Social rights, human rights, the maintenance of cultural goods and the like. The expert report of the export credit agencies also shows that most of the contractually agreed terms are not met. The Austrian government should therefore take an example of the government in Germany, which recently criticized the project very sharply, which is unusually sharp. However, if international standards in Turkey are still not respected by other major projects and social rights and human rights are trampled on, then of course the opportunity for an in-depth economic relationship between Austria and Turkey is minimized, and it is probably the question of how future projects can guarantee compliance with international standards – not only with Ilisu, but also with other projects. To this end, it is likely that there will have to be a stronger execution of existing standards. Only then do I see the opportunity to deepen the relations between these two countries. – Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-AT_2008-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00063_d8e17188	Madam President. Mr Umweltminister! (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) I think all of us were scared accordingly on Wednesday in the early evening and were shocked when an alarm from Slovenia arrived at the EU Commission, so that for the first time EU nuclear alarm was triggered with the content that had run out of the primary cooling circuit in Slovenia in the Krško nuclear power station. This EU-wide alarm system has been available since Chernobyl. It was installed in order to ensure that, in the event of cross-border radioactive contamination, all Member States can be informed as quickly as possible and respond accordingly. We know from history that this was very late at the time and that many of the damage that occurred could have been avoided. The Slovenian side was already made clear a few hours later that it was not a real serious case, but that warning was given. Fortunately, it was not an atomic emergency. Nevertheless, the European public was largely shocked. Once again, there was a bright outcome, not a question, but it was also a test run for all of us to see what happens when something happens, or to see what doesn't happen. It is open to a number of major questions which one must and should talk about here in Parliament too. There are obvious blatant gaps in the information system. Once again, the process has been brought to the fore: At 3 p.m., the leak is discovered. It is the primary cycle, i.e. it is the cooling cycle, which is responsible for the fact that the reactor core always remains at a certain temperature and does not go beyond. The Slovenian authorities need 2 hours 38 minutes to inform Brussels. It will take another 25 minutes for the EU alert to arrive at the Austrian Ministry of the Interior. And then it takes another time, only 3 hours 49 minutes after the leak is discovered in the primary cooling cycle, the main competent radiation warning centre in Austria is informed at the Ministry of the Environment. The Austrian population actually learns all this from the German media, friends from Germany have called and asked: You know something happened? The Austrians have thus experienced this even before the opinion of the Minister for the Environment from German media. But the most important point: There will be a very, very relevant information across Europe, and the really crucial point, namely the radiation warning centre in the Ministry of Environment, which has the task of evaluating what has actually happened and what one can do in this situation, how to protect the Austrian population, what measures can be taken immediately, this radiation warning centre will actually be informed only 3 hours 49 minutes after the incident! And that's the point we need to talk about. No question, there are serious failures on the part of the Slovenian authorities, but – and this is the point on which your responsibility as an environmental minister begins, Mr Pröll – Mr Pröll: Why is this possible at all, why is not a direct link between the nuclear power plant operator, between the one that First of all, has the information given bindingly to the Austrian neighbours who know what to do to protect the population? [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] On question 2: The more time it takes to have a real impact on the population in Austria, the more and more effective – that is clear – first protection measures can be taken. It is therefore essential that information on an accident be passed on as quickly as possible to those responsible in all the countries concerned. The fact that the transmission of the information during the current incident in the Krško AKW to the beam warning centre of the Austrian Ministry of the Environment took almost four hours would undoubtedly not be satisfactory in the event of a serious accident. On the one hand, the shortcomings in communication that have occurred will be securely addressed at both the bilateral and the European and international levels. However, it may be noted that we have installed a security system, regardless of the active reporting of our neighbours. Austria has its own direct online access to the radiation measuring station near Krško, so that in case of increased measurement values we have ensured direct information within a short time – around the clock – because we do not only want to rely on unilateral information. Question 3: All messages received agree that the leak on the 4th. It was discovered at 15.07 a.m. in Central European summer time (13:07 UTC). Question 4: Already yesterday, 5th. June, I mentioned the matter – you know – to the European Environment Council in front of the assembly of the Environment Ministers, not only bilaterally, and also handed over a protest note to my Slovenian colleague Podobnik, with the request for detailed written information and sketch for further action with Slovenia. I have asked him to take all the measures necessary to ensure that such pans will remain in the future or will no longer be present. In addition, I have already said that we expect complete information in bilateral exchanges. Question 5: I have already answered this with question 2. On question 6: The experts of the Ministry of the Environment have the obligation to review and evaluate the information available to them comprehensively and serially before it is passed on to other bodies and to the public, along with the assessment and any recommendation for action, which may also be of great scope – hopefully never. In the present case, my experts have carried out an immediate analysis – I have said this at the time – of the reports received and the review of the current measurement values from the Slovenian radiation early warning system in order to properly assess the situation despite the contradictions in the information – marking as “exercise” in the reporting to the neighbouring countries and to the IAEA, contrary to what has happened internationally across the European Union. As a result, after 25 minutes, the appropriate assessment was made that there has never been a risk to Austria. Questions 7 and 8: The bilateral agreements concern the dissemination of information between the authorities responsible. These are the nuclear supervisory authorities of the operator states and the Federal Ministry of the Interior and the Ministry of the Environment in Austria. The operator of the nuclear installations shall be obliged to inform the competent supervisory authority without delay in the event of an event. It shall assess the effects outside the installation and shall provide for the measures that may be necessary. Therefore, the supervisory authority, but not the CFC operator, has a comprehensive level of information. On the question 9: As already stated on question 6, the Ministry of the Environment has to review the information available and then inform the population in detail according to the situation. In this context, recommendations for action should also be made. This has happened in the present case. Question 10: In accordance with the decision of the Council of the EU, all Member States shall be informed without delay in the event of accidents in installations where the release of radioactive substances may be significant. An analogy agreement also includes the bilateral agreements between Austria and the neighbouring countries: In the event of a radiological danger in which the danger of the neighbouring State cannot be safely excluded, the partner State shall be informed immediately, at the latest, of any decision on measures to protect or inform its own population. On the question 11: ==References====External links== The Temelín truck, which is part of the Temelín truck, is the leakage of about three cubic meters of slightly contaminated water. This has been collected in intended areas, the surface of which has been contaminated between three and eight becquerels per square centimeter. Neither were persons of the Temelín truck, nor any areas outside the power plant, nor were they affected by the Austrian population and the environment. All information provided by the Czech side could be confirmed directly by Austrian measuring stations in the immediate vicinity of the Temelín site, which is true again in the specific case. The info hotline agreed in the milker process has worked in accordance with the agreement. The consequence of this – as in the previous event – is that the Austrian population can rely on the Ministry of the Environment and the radiation early warning system we operate. Dangers are to be named and shown by name, but panic is certainly insidious. Questions 12 and 13: Measures to protect the population in the event of heavy road accidents depend on numerous factors, such as accident in the plant and weather situation. As already explained, therefore, as rapid and comprehensive information from the country of the accident is of utmost importance. In order to ensure this, bilateral cooperation and the exchange of information with all the neighbouring countries of Austria operating nuclear power plants have been considerably expanded in recent years. A permanent automatic data exchange of emergency data, such as measurement values of the radiation early warning systems, ensures the immediate information of the Austrian authorities in an event, which allows the timely initiation and implementation of any measures to protect the population even in relatively short notice periods. In addition, the use of so-called decision-making aid systems allows for initial forecasts of the potential impact of the population by an accident in a nuclear plant to be calculated before an actual release of radioactivity, thus gaining valuable time for the preparation of measures. With regard to the evacuation of the population, it should be noted that, on behalf of my department, extensive analyses have been carried out on the impact of various serious – also very unlikely – road accidents in border installations. These show that large-scale evacuation of the population in Austria will not be necessary in the most unfavourable case. On the other hand, other protective measures, such as the intake of potassium iodit tablets or remaining in the homes, would be to be taken. Preparations have been made for these measures. Questions 14 and 16: These issues of civil protection do not fall within the competence of my department. On question 17: The message also from the “Argumentarium, which I initiated and published: It is clear and clear that nuclear energy, climate protection and sustainability: Nuclear power plants can only be protected to a limited extent from terrorist attacks. Nuclear re-proliferation with the aim of building nuclear weapons continues to be a serious topic in international policy – worldwide. My house has always been concerned with external hazards of nuclear plants. I therefore turned to them immediately after the appointment of the EU coordinator for the fight against terrorism and asked him to pay particular attention to terrorism against nuclear installations. Austria is actively supporting bilateral, international and EU-level activities in this area. Thus, intensive dialogue was conducted with Germany in relation to both intermediate and nuclear power plants – partly in its own special meetings – in which strictly confidential information was also discussed in depth. With regard to the consequences for the specific case of a terrorist attack on a nuclear power plant, it should be said that the nature of the event for a large-scale radioactive contamination in Austria is secondary in emergency planning. The level of radiation exposure and the size of the area concerned are crucial for the necessary measures. In this sense, the effects of terrorist actions do not take a special position in the imaginable scenarios. Questions 18 and 19: Austria was one of the most widely affected countries of Central Europe in the Chernobyl nuclear power plant. In our closer and further surroundings there are numerous nuclear power plants in East and West. However, realistically, an exit from nuclear energy will hardly be possible – even if we represent it. I say clearly and clearly: That won't be possible! The consistent position of Austria is therefore to continue to support every exit from nuclear energy, bearing in mind the risks of nuclear energy, while at the same time continuing initiatives at European level to create uniform high safety standards for nuclear power plants still in operation. However, it should be noted that decisions on national energy policy are largely subject to national sovereignty. What we use for ourselves – this was also demonstrated in the negotiations on Austria's accession to the European Union: national sovereignty in energy policy – we must also admit to others. However, we will have to continue our work of conviction, yes, to continue. Exit scenarios can only be developed together with the State concerned. Questions 20 to 25: Austria continues to reject the energetic use of nuclear power – among other things, because it is not compatible with the principles of sustainable development or is a cost-effective and sustainable option to combat climate change – but unfortunately must respect the national sovereignty of other countries on this issue. However, where it concerns legitimate protection needs of the Austrian population and the protection of the environment, Austria is entitled and obliged to raise its voice. This is also clearly reflected in the government programme for this legislative period, which, among other things, states that Austria will continue to take advantage of and use in all cases of cross-border nuclear power projects under EU law and the ESPOO Convention to protect Austrian safety needs. This attitude is known both to the Government of the Slovak Republic and to that of Italy. On your question of dealing with Mochovce: This also applies to the specific case of the planned completion of blocks 3 and 4 of the Mochovce vehicle. Nevertheless, we do not fail to make clear our position clearly and clearly. It is only yesterday that Mrs Plassnik, in Pressburg, stressed once again this attitude unmistakably. In the light of a genuine construction authorisation, no environmental impact assessment is required in accordance with Slovak law. There is no concrete evidence to date that Slovak law in this area violates EU law or international conventions. Should such new aspects arise, these would, of course, be subject to careful further examination immediately. Nevertheless, I also addressed my Slovak counterparts in writing and, among other things, my urgent wish for the greatest possible information and consultation and for a comprehensive public participation, both in the Slovak Republic and in the Republic of Austria. In his reply, the Slovak Environment Minister points out that the legal situation regarding environmental impact assessment under national and international legislation is currently being thoroughly examined. As there are still no legally binding European safety standards, blocks 3 and 4 of the Mochovce vehicle cannot be measured by binding safety standards. Without prejudice to this, Austria shall also push for maximum safety level and a comprehensive bilateral security dialogue in the event of the planned completion of blocks 3 and 4 of the Mochovce vehicle. The Slovak Republic has already expressed its willingness to do so. The first step will be to clarify the genuinely intentioned retrofitting, since only then will a well-founded assessment be made whether the completion of blocks 3 and 4 of the Mochovce vehicle within the meaning of the EIA Directive or the ESPOO Convention will be possible to qualify as new projects. This is also an essential condition for the European Commission to be consulted. The current procedure under Article 41 of the Euratom Treaty only examines aspects of investment projects related to the objectives of the Euratom Treaty. Security issues are not the subject of this examination. Other Member States, including Austria, have no right to comment in this procedure. Anyone who claims otherwise should be more detailed about the Euratom Treaty. On questions 26 to 28: As I have already done this morning: Yes, the ‘Brussels Agreement’ of November 2001 is a contract of international law. The opinion commissioned and also published by the Federal Government makes this clear. The communication that Austria does not consider the ‘Agreement of Brussels’ as regards the commercial commissioning of the Temelén car to be fulfilled was made with a joint letter from the Federal Chancellor and me on 4 April. ==References== Even if it is not my job, I would point out that Austria has clearly expressed its views in the context of the work of the Temelín Joint Parliamentary Commission. The expediency of a further opinion will be assessed by the Federal Chancellor. Politically, however, it is a matter of implementing the Brussels Agreement to a point and a point. This is what I am working for, while others in this House obviously think that a positive contribution should be made by refusing to take part in the debate in the Parliamentary Commission. The opposite is the case, ladies and gentlemen. On question 29: The conclusion agreements require a common European appearance. This applies particularly to first generation reactors, for example those in Russia or Armenia. For this we work together with several European partners. At present, however, it is mainly about ensuring compliance with the already agreed closure obligations. Even if it is sometimes transparent political motives to challenge agreed closure obligations, it is decided to oppose this. This has also been made clear by the Foreign Minister in Pressburg yesterday. The closure obligations are not negotiable for Austria. Question 30: I recall that from the beginning of its EU membership, Austria has supported reform efforts on the Euratom Treaty and has itself repeatedly launched initiatives to reform this Treaty, in particular in order to eliminate the objective of promoting it, to increase its protection objective, to create fair competition and to democratise decision-making processes. Following the most intensive lobbying of Austria in 2004, five of the 25 Member States back then supported a Constitutional Treaty Declaration calling for a revision conference. There was a need for strong lobbying in order to incorporate this declaration into the Lisbon Treaty. On the one hand, this has shown that Austria is not alone with this effort, but on the other hand it has shown quite clearly that the majority required for the establishment of an Intergovernmental Conference, in particular, does not appear to have the unanimity required to amend the Euratom Treaty in Europe. We must therefore continue to focus our efforts on expanding and strengthening the support base for reform. On questions 31 and 32: Changed circumstances require new political responses. Politics is still the art of the possible. As the debate on a renaissance of nuclear energy began, I commissioned the nuclear forum – the relevant scientific advisory body of the Austrian Federal Government – to examine the potential contribution of nuclear energy to the fight against climate change – an international argument – and to a sustainable energy future in detail. This examination requires several years of hard work. The merger of highly specialized knowledge of different disciplines in a publication as well as the preparation of this knowledge in a generally understandable yet scientifically sound form places high demands on all parties involved. The result of this analysis, entitled “Climate Energy, Climate Change and Sustainability” – I have mentioned this – has been in existence since 2007 and is also of great interest at European and international level. I have already sent a copy of this analysis to all Members of the National Council a year ago. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! ParlaMint-AT_2008-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00063_d8e20189	Mr President. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) You have outlined – and I can only agree with you – that the noise in its various forms is not only a nuisance, but – as far as quality of life and health threats are concerned – a very serious challenge. We have accepted this challenge, with various elements that are given to us to ensure that in Austria, with regard to noise pollution, it will be better. The prevention of noise is an essential element of environmental policy in Austria and is also prominently anchored in the Federal Constitutional Act on comprehensive environmental protection. However, there is also a positive trend – that is already seen from the measures – which can be read. First, the last environmental monitoring report of the Federal Environment Agency shows a gratifying long-term trend, namely that the proportion of the population affected by noise has a sharp decline in trend. In 1970 more than 50 percent of the population felt troubled by noise, and in 2003 this figure was only more than 30 percent. – To avoid misunderstanding: That's still too much, but the trend and the development is right. But of course it is not enough if almost a third of the Austrians in this area still see the need for action. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Dwarf.]] Where is the main need for action? - You mentioned that. This is certainly the transport sector. Around 73 percent of those concerned see the main cause of noise pollution in the transport sector. On the side of the Ministry of Life, we have taken the right step with the Federal Environment Protection Act. Legal implementation must now be carried out by the federal states in order to record noise levels in a uniform manner and then to take the appropriate measures. It is only to be seen – and this is incredible about what you have said on the subject of motorcycle noise pollution – that the implementation of the European Environmental Noise Directive in the first stage is mainly aimed at high-loaded sections of the road, with a traffic volume of more than 6 million vehicles per year. The classic motorcycle routes and your example with the old woman will not fit into this category. It is only in the second stage, starting in 2012, that all roads with more than 3 million vehicles per year will be recorded. You have carried out what my ministerial colleagues and country leaders have written to you. It is indeed true that noise pollution cannot be reduced by individual measures. This requires a mosaic of various measures, namely: targeted change of consciousness, speed limits, eco- Nomic driving – which must also be taught in the driving schools – focussed on the fact that the technical condition of the motorcycles and so on is subject to a test according to precise specifications. By the way – you have also mentioned this – noise protection is a classical cross-sectional matter between federal and state legislators. ParlaMint-AT_2008-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00063_d8e21034	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! I'd like to remind you briefly of what this is all about. In 2001, the compensation fund law was passed in compliance with the Washington Agreement. The purpose is to enable victims of the Nazi regime who have not been or have not been adequately compensated to claim their claims. The second part of the law regulates the natural restitution of properties which have been withdrawn between 1938 and 1945 and are owned by the federal government. Countries and municipalities have also been given the opportunity to use the arbitration agency established in § 38. Since some municipalities have only recently taken advantage of this possibility or only plan to use it, deadlines are extended, deadlines which expired at the end of 2007. This is not an error, as Mr Aspöck thinks. It is therefore not a matter of correcting something, but of continuing to allow the arbitration body to deal with requests for restitution. And I think that's actually in all of our minds. Accordingly, the President of the National Council and the Second President made requests and we reached an agreement in the Committee. There were four of them. Parties to make arrangements. We have also established a committee that, if it turns out in the process that these are federal entities, of course they should also be dealt with. That means I'm convinced that's a round thing that's in the sense of the victims. And I am glad that there is a very broad agreement here.
ParlaMint-AT_2008-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00063_d8e21080	The next speaker is Mr Dr. Sonnberger. Also 2 minutes of voluntary time limit. - You're talking.
ParlaMint-AT_2008-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00063_d8e21101	High house! The deadline for the application of the compensation fund law in the case of natural restitutions of state or municipality properties should initially be extended generally until the end of 2009. Furthermore, it is fixed that the application period is at least two years from the date on which the respective local authority makes use of an opt-in according to § 38. This is in absolute numbers an additional need of about €4 billion. So far, we assumed that this is still feasible in the sustainability path. But what happens if life expectancy increases even more than is assumed in this path? What happens if the economy is developing worse than it is assumed in this path? – In such a case, it makes sense to develop an emergency plan. Even companies, when they create emergency plans, do not do so if a disaster has already occurred, but do so in good times. That is how we have done this in the government programme and in the government decision of 11. Januaryen to introduce such an improved sustainability, and I think that further discussions and a strengthening of the role of Parliament can also help to find a good way forward – just as you will today make a good decision for more than two million pensioners in our country with the advanced pension adjustment on 1.11.2008.

ParlaMint-AT_2008-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00063_d8e24875	Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to refer to the petition number 18 concerning the inclusion of HPV vaccination in the children's vaccination programme. She was introduced to Parliament last July by fellow Members Christine Muttonen and Melitta Trunk. In an accompanying letter from the country of Carinthia, the importance of this prevention measure was stressed and advocated for the widest possible access to this HPV vaccination. The Committee on Petitions has asked the Ministry of Health for an opinion and the opinion received is based on the December 2007 report of the Ludwig-Boltzmann Institute. “HPV” is the abbreviation for “human papillomaviruses” responsible for the disease of cervical cancer. In 2003, there were 473 diseases and 169 deaths in Austria. By comparison, 34 000 diseases and 14 000 deaths in the EU in the same year. Overall, mortality has decreased by the early detection precautions in the Western countries and in Austria, and there has been a vaccination against this infection since 2006. The approved vaccines act against four types of viruses that cause 75 percent of cancer. With this vaccination, the hope of reducing the number of new diseases in the longer term is understandable. However, some suspicions of side effects of these vaccines insecure the population. I would like to point out here once again the importance of the precautions which cannot be replaced by vaccination. In conclusion, the opinion of the Ministry of Health states that vaccinations can only be included in the vaccination programme after a positive medical and socio-economic evaluation in the federal, state and the main association of social insurance institutions. We will continue to observe where the way is going in this matter. Thank you.

[[It was in favour of SPÖ and Greens as well as of representatives of ÖVP and BZÖ.]] That is why I would like to explain a few really central points to you in the near future, as you have obviously not been exposed to the study of this law. Originally, it was about implementing an EU directive on access for men and women to services. What has been achieved together, especially with Mrs. State Secretary Marek in the Ministry of Economic Affairs, is that today, beyond the implementation of this directive, we can make significant improvements in the equal treatment law. From now on, provided that the House of Lords also decides today, and gratefully so, because of the Committee debate and the discussion here, in the future, we have the opportunity to fight not only the resolution in the case of relations of employment which have been dissolved because of discrimination – because many women do not want to return to a workplace where they have been sexually harassed – but that they will have the option to either challenge the termination of the service relationship or claim damages in the future. As far as minimum claims for damages are concerned, we have also used this novel to increase these claims. In addition, we have also said that if there are multiple discriminations, which must also be taken into account in compensation for damages. We have also extended the limitation periods because we know that, in a dependency relationship between the employer and the employee, we often need time to process such experiences and then see: what legal steps can I take? This is very important for people affected. We have, of course, also admitted – and I would like to address this – that there is discrimination at the end of those working conditions that are temporary or working conditions that are still in the probationary period. I think it is very important that we take this into account, too, because I believe that no one employed in a holding may be exposed to harassment and discrimination, whether it be a temporary service contract or an unlimited service contract. If it comes to an end because of discrimination, people will have to learn the protection of the law, and they will have it if we decide on this law today. [[It was in favour of SPÖ and Greens as well as representatives of the ÖVP.]] I believe that it is possible to further improve the really good law on equal treatment, which we have in Austria to protect people from discrimination. I would also like to refer very briefly to the fact that I support, on my part and on my part, a campaign which is quite clearly on the side of the victims, when it comes to violence against women and children in this country. Whenever there are events that are so dramatic that they come to the public very strongly, everyone proves that there must be a better victim protection. But if we say that we have laws that protect victims in terms of housing – and this is the core of the Austrian anti-violence laws – there is again criticism. If we say in Austria: not the victim must complain to the perpetrator, but the state is behind the victim – and that is in large numbers, 94 percent, women and their children – then that is right. It is quite right that this House has always said quite clearly: there is no tolerance for violence against women and children, that is not a cavalier offence, which can be spoken of indignantly from the speaker's mouth, but it is a very serious violation of human rights when women and children are violently committed in their own four walls. In 1999, there were 41 national air traffic controllers that monitored the air sovereignty of the national states. Every flight across Europe has resulted in frequent change of control stations. That is why the creation of a single European sky is of great importance and provides a major relief for European air transport. In addition to facilitating air traffic, the creation of a single European airspace means ensuring a high level of safety for air traffic. The most important measures to guarantee air safety are: Firstly, the Europe-wide approximation of requirements for air safety service providers to national supervisory authorities. The Court of Auditors' concrete recommendations are as follows: Less railway crossings, more barriers, better visible safeguard measures and information on road users. The BMVIT has therefore created a list of 56 particularly dangerous intersections, called hotspots. Minister Faymann has taken immediate measures for every hotspot to disincentive the situation. In 2007, 90 railway crossings were opened, and in 2008 another 36 crossings were opened. The Court of Auditors also called for the railway crossing regulation to be urgently updated. Here too, Minister Faymann is active. A regulation adapted to changes in road transport is expected to be expected next spring. Ladies and gentlemen! You see, we are on a good way to further improve safety on the sideways. Thank you.
